# Continuity after Volume 06 Batch 0001 (Chapters 251 to 260)

**Additive to every section above. VOLUME 06 IS OPEN. The volume plan is `outline/volume-06.md`, the canon contract for the block is `outline/batches/volume-06-batch-0001.md`, and the block record is `state/volume-06-batch-0001-summary.md`.**

## The instruments, and the two this block added

- **A PRINTED SHEET ON A GATEPOST IS THE BLOCK'S FIRST INSTRUMENT AND IT IS SOMEBODY ELSE'S PAPER.** Nailed at about the height of a man's shoulder, fresh nail, no mud on it, put there by nobody, found about a quarter to twelve on the first of the sixth month. It names one building and no place and no person and carries one number and one day. **A building is not a place name, and a clerk entered on the page that the sheet says the Hall and does not say which Hall.**
- **THE BLOCK'S SECOND INSTRUMENT IS A SECOND BOOK IN THE ROOM WHERE THE BOOKS ARE.** Bound ledger of the same make as the first, about three-quarters of an inch thick, on the shelf on its side with a second chair turned up on top of it so that nobody coming in at a hurry can knock it off. **THE THIRD COLUMN OF THE COVENANT IS STILL RULED AND EMPTY AND THE NINTH CHARTER'S LINE IS STILL EMPTY AND NEITHER WAS FILLED IN ANY OF THESE TEN CHAPTERS, AND THE THIRD COLUMN OF THE COVENANT AND THE THIRD COLUMN OF ANYTHING ELSE IN THIS BLOCK ARE NOT THE SAME COLUMN AND WERE NOT JOINED.**
- **THE REST OF THE INSTRUMENT THE BLOCK FOUND IS PHYSICAL AND WAS FOUND BY LOOKING AT THE BACK OF A BUILDING.** A plank door in the end wall of a room about two hundred yards down the lane behind the bank, with a hasp on the outside and a nail in the hasp instead of a staple; it comes off its nails in about two seconds, makes a noise like a duck, and goes down flat in a ditch that is about four inches deep in the dry and about four feet in the wet. **About three feet of the rope on an oak frame four miles out past the loams is gone and nine inches of stub is left inside a beam eleven feet off the ground, and a woman of about thirty-one who keeps a route said out loud that whoever untied it did not want the bell gone, they wanted it to be there and to be unreachable, and that that is a thing somebody thought about for a long time.**
- **A ROOM WITH NOTHING OVER THE DOOR IS THE FOURTH INSTRUMENT AND IT HAS THREE TRADES AND A BENCH.** A room about nine feet by fourteen about two miles the other way on a lane with no name on anything, kept by a woman of about twenty-nine for four years, with about nine people in it most nights. It trades where about nineteen people slept last night, which lane is under water where, and the fact that a person has been in a place. **Nobody in that room raised their voice between about nine in the evening and about half past ten, and a clerk entered that she was not in the room and knows that from the woman of about twenty-nine and not from being there. There are about eleven notches cut in the underside of the seat at intervals that are not even and they are not a calendar and are not a count of nights and are not a count of people and the woman who keeps the room said so before anybody asked her.**

## The people, and what changed in the block

- **The man the figure of twenty-one years is against.** **Said the block's central sentence about himself, out loud, in a yard, and was told the volume's sentence by a man of fifty-six, and did not do anything about either.** Offered a woman of about thirty-one a sheet with her name under a line at six in the morning without asking her first what she was doing that month, and was refused in about four seconds on his own rule said back to him. Asked the registrar for the number of names in the second book in her own doorway and was refused in about four seconds. **He is a Convener, is not a founder, is not a holder, is not a bearer of a toll, is not a party of the fourth of four, is not on the standing orders of the nine, and is findable.**
- **The registrar of this district's records office.** **BROKE HER OWN RULE OF THE TWENTY-FIRST OF THE MONTH BEFORE LAST AND SAID SO OUT LOUD AT ABOUT HALF PAST TWO IN THE AFTERNOON OF THE SIXTH OF THIS MONTH.** A second book, in the same room, on the same table and the shelf, in her hand, looked at by nobody else. She is not a bearer, not a holder, not a steward and not a party of anything, and **a name put on a figure cannot be taken off it by the person it is put on is the rule that protects her as much as it catches him, and she says so.** **She was asleep at a desk at about four in the morning on the seventh and her right hand would not close on a pen for about nine seconds, and she woke up and closed it and said nothing about it for the rest of the day.**
- **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing.** **Said in a yard that he had been wrong about five months of work and had found it out at the back of a building.** Refused to read the rival record out an eighth time, with a reason, on the morning it took effect. Could not get his fingers round the nail in a hasp on the first and it took him about nine seconds, and could not open a gate on the first and did not try again on the second, and could not get a board back into swollen nail holes and a man who was not asked held it for him.
- **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam.** **Said the block's finding, which is a subtraction he refused to call a finding.** Refused the same join a fourth time and the thirteen and the twelve did not move. Put his right hand flat on the sill under a foot and a half of water on the second and could not close it for the rest of the afternoon, and had not said so and said nothing on the third. Disagreed with the first panel out loud. Tied nothing for anybody in this block and was the man who held a board.
- **The man of fifty-six.** **Said the volume's sentence: you have been standing next to a thing and calling it representing it. Standing next to a thing is not standing in its place. And he said he was saying it to that man because the man had found it out himself that morning and he was not going to be a man who takes a thing off a man who has just found it out on his own.** Said that his own rule is worse than a printed one because a printed one needs nobody in the room. Refused eleven coppers a week for the eighth time. Stood at a rise a mile out past the loams and looked at a building eleven miles off for about nine minutes and said one sentence about not being frightened of it.
- **The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale.** **SHE IS A WOMAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-SIX AND SHE HAS STOOD AT THAT SCALE NINE YEARS AND WEIGHS ABOUT NINE SACKS A DAY, AND SHE IS THE PERSON WHO GOT THE KEEPERS' WORD OUT OF A STRANGER'S SCHEDULE BY HEARING A COLUMN HEADED OPEN AND ASKING WHAT WAS OPENING, AND THE REASON THIS DISTRICT HAS THE KEEPERS' WORD INSTEAD OF THE REGISTRY'S IS THAT A WOMAN AT A WEIGHBRIDGE HEARD A HEADING AND ASKED WHAT IT MEANT. KEEP THAT.** Refused her four empty houses again before she was asked, on the day a season began. Stood eleven hours at that scale on the fourth and could not get her right arm above her shoulder afterwards and had to take the beam down with her left hand. Said the block's line about sacks: a man with a paper is carrying a thing she could not lift.
- **The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally for six households.** **HE IS A MAN OF ABOUT FORTY-EIGHT AND NOT THIRTY-FOUR. Volume 05 said thirty-four in fourteen chapters; the closed canon of Volumes 03 and 04 says forty-eight in ten chapters and he says of himself in Chapter 184 that he is fifty-one.** One signed, two refused, three not asked again, and the figure was printed at forty-one on the first, forty-four on the fourth, forty-seven on the seventh and fifty on the tenth, and **on the fourth and the seventh and the tenth he said out loud that he was going to say it once and not again this month, and a clerk entered that the figure is his and was not asked for.**
- **The man of about thirty-four who keeps a road.** Refused a second thing in a yard on the grounds that he has been asked one question in this district since the first of the month that is now last month and has given one answer. **Said out loud on a rise that there is a gate about a mile before the place where the ground stops and that everybody in this basin thinks the gate is the far end of the road, and that the road goes on for another seven miles and he has not walked the last of it in six years and was not going to that day, and that nobody has ever asked him what is at the end of it and he would like that written down as a thing nobody has asked him.** He is not a bearer of the road and has never been asked to be one.
- **The woman of about thirty-four who keeps a goat, at the third house up the lane from the scale.** Held a slate for nine minutes on the fourth for a woman who could not lift her arm and could not get her hand round the edge of it at the end, and said no to a second thing before she was asked, in a printed span of twelve words: **I AM NOT A SECOND HAND AND I WILL NOT BE ONE**
- **The woman of about thirty-one who keeps a route for people who are not in any book.** **NEW TO THIS BLOCK, AND THE TRADE IS A TRADE AND NOT A NAME.** Came down four miles in the dark, refused a name under a line in about four seconds on the ground that a name under a line is a door, refused to say how many people there are, said the route does not pass the frame, took the skin off the inside of her left shin about the length of two fingers on the wire on the left of the lane behind the bank and said nothing about it for two days, and walked about nine people out on the routes at night in fours and fives with a man of about thirty-four who keeps a road in front of them who was not asked to be. **She is the only person in this basin who has ever found him without being sent, and she had not been down that lane in eleven months, and she is his sister, and that fact is on no page in this district and has never been on one.**
- **The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room with nothing over the door.** **NEW TO THIS BLOCK.** She is the room and not a person in it. She said no before she was asked, in a hundred and seventy-three words, to the fifth of the five things this district does not have, and she refused a list of nine houses in the room by proxy, in a hundred and nine words, and the reason she gave is that a man who copies for nothing would not have sold it and that is why he is not to have it.
- **The man of about twenty-three who sells nothing and copies for nothing.** **Asked for eleven coppers a week for the eighth time in this block, in Chapter 255, and was refused for the eighth time, and the district has now said no eight times. HE MADE NO COPY OF THE REMOVAL SHEET.** He was refused a copy of a sheet with nothing on it in 253 and a list of nine houses in 257, both with a reason, and he made no copy of anything at all on the seventh.
- **The woman of fifty-eight.** Came down the bank with two buckets as she has since the seventeenth of last month, and **nobody counted the buckets on any of the ten days and a clerk entered that nobody did and that a bucket is about eleven pounds full and that nobody in this basin has ever asked her.** **On the fourth of this month she was at the top of the bank at about ten minutes to four in the morning with a lamp and a bucket, because she comes down at six most mornings and had come down at four, and she saw the nine of them come the last two hundred yards in fours and fives and put the lamp down and picked it up again and did not say one word to anybody, and the record about the lamp and the two hundred yards says not asked.** **The ninth of the eleven houses she carries to has a woman in it who is being moved on the first of the month after next.**
- **The man of about fifty-five who keeps a building four miles down a lane.** Sent nothing to the yard on any of the ten days and nobody sent anything to him. The tank is still about a hand's breadth over its lip, the two chalk lines are still on the inside of the brick, the crack is about the length of his hand, the nine years of beds are still in the second room, and **nobody has asked for a fifth mend in four months and counting.** He is named once and not visited.
- **A man of about nineteen who counts.** **Declined to count a population in a yard with a number nailed to a gatepost in front of him, for the second time in two months, and the first time he has declined on a day when there was a number in front of him.** Counted nineteen people, one nail, two buckets and three lines on a sheet, and said that was everything he could see. Counted two things on a rise and declined the rest. **He is nobody's clerk and is not to be turned into a counter of people.**
- **The man of about thirty at a counter.** **The sixteenth time down the eleven mile road and the next is the seventeenth, and no chapter in this block gives him a day in this district and a clerk entered that on the eighth.**
- **The man of about forty-one who keeps a road house at the top of the road that climbs.** **He has no day in this district, this district has never once given him one, and this block gave him none.** A clerk entered on the eighth that he was standing outside his own door that morning, as he was on the fifteenth and the twentieth of last month, and was not asked a first thing.
- **The man with the portfolio.** **Still not in this district, off the page, and nothing in this block says where he is.**

## The counts, and the six that were defended

**Things this district does not have five, the fifth being a way to pay a person who is not in a household, and it was named out loud in a room with nothing over the door and refused, and it is still not paid and not priced. Instruments built and not named six, and a seventh was NOT proposed in ten days and six candidates were refused on the page. Documents nobody owns three, and three documents arrived or were made in this block and none became a fourth; THE REMOVAL SHEET IS ENTERED AS A FOURTH DOCUMENT THAT MENTIONS NO OFFICE AND THAT IS THE OFFICE'S OWN LIST AND IT DID NOT MOVE. Protected things five and no sixth, and a building with two doors was used twice as a thing that is not a sixth. Conditions with no end on it four. Refusals to join two things thirteen and the registrar's own count twelve, and neither moved, and a join was refused a fourth time and a join is a join. Refusals to read nine, the departure unspent, no date in the ledger, the fourth line of the offer not read out, and the rival record is not the fourth line of the offer. Boards five and lines full six, a seventh ruled and empty, and nobody put a line on a board on any of the seventy-one days to the tenth of this month, counting the first of the fourth month in. Columns of not-askings four and no fifth proposed. A rate turning a year into coppers: none, and there never has been one. Readings of the rival record seven, and the eighth was refused out loud on the first. Four columns of not-askings, a chalk figure on a board, a set of doors counted in chalk and a subtraction of a printed number and two open pages were all refused on the page as columns, figures or instruments.**

**THE POOL OF REFUSALS WITH NO REASON A CLERK OF A HOUSE HAS GIVEN IS EIGHT AND IT STOOD WHERE IT WAS ON EVERY ONE OF THE TEN DAYS AGAINST SIX SEPARATE ATTEMPTS.** Six people refused something with a reason and not one of them is a clerk of a house: **the woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale (251), the man of about thirty-four who keeps a road (252), the woman of about thirty-one who keeps a route (253 and 259), the woman of about thirty-four who keeps a goat (254), the man of about twenty-three who copies for nothing (253, 255 and 257), and the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room with nothing over the door (257).** A clerk entered at the point of each that a refusal with a reason is not one of the eight and that a man or a woman who keeps a house is not a clerk of a house. The refusals about the ninth holding are seven and did not move and the two are not the same figure.

## The counting motif, thirty-seven class-one claims and one class-two claim

**Thirty-seven class-one claims in the class counts 5, 5, 3, 3, 4, 3, 4, 4, 2, 4, and thirty-seven reproduce, with a mismatch count of zero. One class-two claim, twelve words over a printed span of twelve, and it reproduces.** The detector was calibrated on Chapters 241 to 250 first and returns 53 class-one claims, 0 mismatches, a denominator of 25,569, a `wc -w` total of 25,689 and 675 shared twelve-word runs, **all five of which are the figures the Volume 05 records print and all five of which reproduce.** **Six relative-month phrases were wrong in the first draft and all six were corrected by counting the month each one names; two class-one claims moved when the sentences under them were corrected and both were re-measured rather than re-aimed; a class-two claim of eleven over a twelve-word span was corrected to twelve by counting the span and not by shortening it.** **The block total is 25,335 words by `wc -w` and 25,213 on the Batch 0005 denominator, and all ten chapters sit inside the 2,200 to 3,200 band; every figure in this section was re-measured after the review repair.**

## Untouched in this block, to be left untouched

**The blank lot, Iven, the ninth design signature, the origin of the Common Measure, the First House, the removed signature, the three unexplained things of Volume 01, the office, the resolution of the romance, the name of the city on the agent's ledger, the name of the agent in any register in the west bank, the fourth line of the offer, the departure, the boy's name in a column in a building two days of road away, the two figures of five years and nine years, the iron, the echo of an origin toll, the Last Fare, the market stalls on the lower terrace, the first channel's failure and its sills, the four gaps, the two impressions, the two lines holding the office's name, the four lines of the broken circle, the signal's memory pattern, the pan that cannot report itself, the place that cannot report itself, and the mark on the iron. No place is named on any document in this district and none is named in narration, and the country above the water is not named. Cael Orin's name was said once in a yard in Chapter 203 and was not said a second time. Selik Marne is a wary ally and not a friend and is not in this block. THE LOCK COUNCIL AND THE QUIET REGISTRY ARE BODIES THAT PRINT AND DO NOT ARRIVE, AND NEITHER ACQUIRED A FACE. A KEEPER HAS NEVER BEEN ON A PAGE AND THERE NEVER WAS ONE AND NO KEEPER IS NAMED, AND THE WORD OCCURS THIRTEEN TIMES IN THIS BLOCK AND EVERY OCCURRENCE IS A DENIAL, A RULE PRINTED ON A SHEET, OR A COLUMN THAT IS EMPTY. THE NINTH PLACE ON THE PAGE OUT OF THE CAVE IS RULED AND EMPTY AND WAS NOT FILLED. THE BELL WAS NOT RUNG. NO NEW FINAL ENEMY.**

## The world in this block

**A yard a stranger can walk into with a sheet on a gatepost and a chair nobody has sat in for forty-eight days and a gate that will not open. A lane behind the bank, about two hundred yards, a wire on the left not tight since before anybody in this district was born, a step of two stones and a plank door with a hasp and a second door with a handle and a curtain, a third house up from the scale where a woman keeps a goat, a building with two doors at the near end, and a ditch behind it about four feet deep in the wet. A channel at the sluice end of the loams with about a foot above a sill. A scale under a lean-to. A rise a mile out past the loams and a building eleven miles off with a roof that is not a roof you can cover a house with. An oak frame four miles out past the loams with a cracked bell and nine inches of rope. Two walls a mile apart. A room about nine feet by eleven with one table, one shelf, one door, a fanlight over the door that lights nothing and has not in four months, and now two books. A room about nine feet by fourteen with nothing over the door and about eleven notches on the underside of a bench. A kitchen doorway four hundred yards down a lane with no chalk on the step.**

# Continuity after Volume 06 Batch 0002 (Chapters 261 to 270)

**Additive to every section above. VOLUME 06 IS OPEN AND TWO BLOCKS ARE WRITTEN. The volume plan is `outline/volume-06.md`, the canon contracts for the blocks are `outline/batches/volume-06-batch-0001.md` and `outline/batches/volume-06-batch-0002.md`, and the block records are `state/volume-06-batch-0001-summary.md` and `state/volume-06-batch-0002-summary.md`.**

## The instruments, and the three this block added

- **THE BLOCK'S FIRST INSTRUMENT IS A NAIL THAT DID NOT MOVE.** A hasp on a plank door in the end wall of a building of one room at the near end of the lane behind the bank, with a nail in the hasp instead of a staple. **One man worked it on the eleventh of the sixth month, with his left hand and then with his right, and the nail did not move, and the plank door came off anyway with the flat of an axe in about two seconds, and about nine feet of brick with no window in it has been open at the back of that building in and out of this block.** The ditch behind is about two feet deep in the sixth month and about four feet in the wet. **A building with two doors is not a sixth protected thing and a building with a hole in the end wall of it is not a sixth either.**
- **THE BLOCK'S SECOND INSTRUMENT IS A THREE-LINE CARD ON A TABLE.** A man of about forty-four with a case put it on the trestle table of the yard of Lot Seventeen and asked the yard to read it, and the yard read it because a yard reads a document when somebody asks it to read one. **THE RULE OF THE COUNTER WAS USED BY A MAN WHO IS NOT OF THIS DISTRICT TO MAKE HIS OWN OFFER PUBLIC IN ABOUT NINE SECONDS, and a clerk entered that.**
- **THE BLOCK'S THIRD INSTRUMENT IS A FOUR-LINE SHEET ON A GATEPOST.** It says a yard in which things are read out is a yard in which things are kept; that one copy of anything entered in it is to go to the body that sent it; that the column for the person who keeps that yard is empty on the sheet and was empty when it was printed; and that nobody has to be asked. **THE FIRST SHEET NAMED A JOB AND AN EMPTY COLUMN UNDER IT. THE SECOND ONE TOOK THE PERSON OUT OF THE JOB AND PRINTED THE REASON ON THE FACE OF IT, AND A CLERK ENTERED THAT A SHEET WHICH SAYS IN PRINT THAT NOBODY NEED BE ASKED IS NOT A PERSON ASKING.** Both sheets are nailed to the gatepost of that yard and the nail hole of the removal sheet is beside them with nothing in it.
- **A LIST IS THE FOURTH THING THIS BLOCK MADE AND IT IS THE ONLY ONE OF THE FOUR THAT IS A DOCUMENT AND WAS NEVER PRINTED.** A list of the people in this district who are not in a household, written in about an hour on the thirteenth of the sixth month by a man of about twenty-three who copies for nothing, for money. **A man with a case owns it and paid for it, and a clerk entered that it is not one of the three documents this district does not own and is not a seventh instrument of the six, and that no figure in this block is a count of what is on it.** He took it out of this district on the nineteenth.
- **THE SECOND BOOK STANDS WHERE IT STOOD.** Two open pages, forty-one lines, and a hand that carried one name across a page break. **THE FIGURE IS FORTY PEOPLE, AND A CLERK ENTERED THAT A COUNT OF LINES IS NOT A COUNT OF PEOPLE AND THAT A LINE WITH A PERSON'S NAME ON IT IS NOT A PERSON.**

## The people, and what changed in the block

- **The man the figure of twenty-one years is against.** **He was not in the yard of Lot Seventeen on the sixteenth of the sixth month, which is the first of eleven days in that month that he has not stood in it, and nobody asked him why.** He was in it on the seventeenth and said that a yard will hold anything you put in it if you say it out loud in it and that there is no column anywhere in this district that will hold him responsible. **On the eighteenth, in front of about nine people, he offered to say the shape of the second book in the yard without saying one name and she refused him in about four seconds, and he asked her to say it in a room and she told him there is none.** On the twentieth he wrote twenty-four words on a slate and told her not to come and find him about the forty-one, and then said there was bread on the board and he would come back for it at seven. He is a Convener, is not a founder, is not a holder, is not a bearer of a toll, is not a party of the fourth of four, is not on the standing orders of the nine, and is findable.
- **The registrar of this district's records office.** **She refused a man in front of about nine people on the eighteenth of the sixth month and then said the other half of what she meant, which is that there is no room in this district that nobody can walk into.** She was four feet off a trestle table for about nine minutes on the seventeenth while forty-one names were said in a yard and did not stop him, and said out loud that she wrote them on a page at about five in the morning on the sixth of this month and read nothing out and asked nobody and that neither of them was asked. She said *I know what is in it* in a doorway on the sixteenth and asked for three clauses in the minute in her own hand. She could not get her own door open at about half past five on the seventeenth and it took her about nine seconds. She stopped twice in four hundred yards on the eighteenth. **On the twentieth she said she wanted to have had nothing to do with what he is going to do and asked for that to be written down as a thing she asked for and not as a thing he decided.** She is not a bearer, not a holder, not a steward and not a party of anything, and a keeper of a room is not a thing she is.
- **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing.** **He asked a question in a lane to about four people on the sixteenth and the same question in a yard in front of about nineteen people on the seventeenth, and the difference between four people saying nothing and forty-one names was him, standing up in front of more people, and he said so out loud and a clerk entered that nobody argued with him.** He said in a yard on the fourteenth that his left hand has not been right since the first of this month and that a man who asks questions and cannot hold what he is asking about is a man somebody else can use. He asked the woman of fifty-eight the same question five times and it was answered on the fifth. He carried the slate for the fourteenth time. He could not get his fingers round a nail in a hasp on the first of this month, and worked it with both hands and could not shift it.
- **The man of about thirty-four who digs loam.** Went down about two feet into the ditch behind the building with two doors on the eleventh of the sixth month and came up with a hand that does not close and said so out loud to about nine people, having said nothing for nine days. **Counted forty-two lines in daylight on the eighteenth with the registrar holding the book, because he could not turn a page corner with that hand, and said out loud that the arithmetic had been right twice in eleven days and the figure had been wrong twice, and that a man who gets a number right twice by counting lines has been counting lines.** He was not in the yard on the sixteenth and nobody sent for him. He could not get his fingers under the edge of the trestle table on the twentieth and did it with two fingers a minute later.
- **The man of fifty-six.** **Said out loud on the fifteenth of the sixth month, in front of about eleven people, that he had been pleased for seven days about a printed rule being better than his own while a man with a case sat at the end of a table and a man wrote down a list, and that being pleased about it is the reason both of those things could happen in that yard.** He took a pen out of a man's fingers on the thirteenth and said nothing. He said the price of asking a question in front of about nineteen people on the seventeenth, once, and nobody in that yard was able to answer him. He said the three places on the eighteenth. He said the sentence about a person being asked something on the nineteenth. **The descriptor *man of fifty-six* fell from thirty-six in Batch 0001 to twenty-two in this block, and a clerk in the frame table records that the block gives him the two most expensive sentences he has had in five blocks.**
- **The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale.** **She was asked to be the keeper of a building on the eleventh of the sixth month and said no with a reason. She was asked on the twelfth whether she would say more about the ninth of the eleven houses and said no with a reason, and gave the best sentence in the block: a thing said by a person who was not asked is the only thing in this district that is worth nothing, and being asked has made her an answer.** She named the fifth of the five things in a yard on the thirteenth and a man of fifty-six refused it with a reason that is not the shape of the other eight. **She disagreed with a printed rule out loud in a yard on the sixteenth, in the first of its three lines, and accepted the other two, and a clerk entered that she took a risk on a day when anything said in that yard can be asked for a copy of and that nobody asked her to.** Her four empty houses still stand and she said on the twentieth that they are not going to be offered again before the first day of the season after this one.
- **The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally for six households.** **HE IS A MAN OF ABOUT FORTY-EIGHT AND NOT THIRTY-FOUR. On the fifteenth of the sixth month a clerk asked him for his figure for the first time in five months and he refused with a reason that is new: a figure of mine in a yard where a man with a case is sitting is a figure of mine in his list, and he does not have to ask me a second time, and I am not going to be a man who has to be asked twice.** The printed figures are fifty-one on the eleventh, fifty-five on the fifteenth and sixty on the nineteenth, and a clerk has the figure without asking and entered that the figure is his and that a figure a man will not repeat is not a refusal and is not a consent.
- **The man of about thirty-four who keeps a road.** Refused a second thing on the twelfth. Walked his four miles on the fifteenth and told the nine houses on it about the man with a case and not one of the nine asked him for it. **On the nineteenth he came into the yard and said that one of the nine went and found the man, and that he was not ashamed of it and was right not to be, and that he had stood in that yard on the fifteenth and told a man about a list without ever once asking him whether his house was on one.** He is not a bearer of the road and has never been asked to be one.
- **The woman of about thirty-four who keeps a goat, at the third house up the lane from the scale.** Said no to a third thing before she was asked on the fourteenth, in a printed span of **nineteen** words, and would not say it twice: **I HAVE SAID NO TO A MAN WITH A CASE AND HE IS NOT A MAN WITH A SLATE**
- **The woman of about thirty-one who keeps a route for people who are not in any book.** **She refused a third time in about four seconds on the fourteenth of the sixth month and gave the volume's own reason: if a man is hauling sacks for a man with a case then that man is a person who is not in a household by the ordinary meaning of those words, and if his name is on a list then a route is a thing somebody can buy, and she has spent nine years making sure there is nothing in this basin that can be bought that walks.** She came down four miles on the seventeenth because a man had come down her route in the morning and had not been one of hers, and said one thing that was not a refusal. She is not a bearer of a route, has never been asked to be one, and is not a keeper.
- **The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room with nothing over the door.** **She was found by a man with a case because a woman at a house on that lane told him where the room was, and she refused him in about four seconds in her own room on the fourteenth and told him he could have the bench, and said out loud that everything she had said on the seventh of this month was right and none of it was any use, and that the list he was holding was made before she knew he was on that lane, and that this was the first sum of four years of arithmetic that came out wrong.** She is not a clerk of a house and not a bearer of a bench.
- **The man of about forty-four with a case.** **NEW TO THE PROSE, and the one planned antagonist this block spends, and he is a method and not a face.** He came into the yard of Lot Seventeen at about ten in the morning on the thirteenth of the sixth month, said what he was in the first minute, asked the yard to read his own card, and was given the end of a trestle table. He got a list out of a man of about twenty-three. **He lost it in about nine minutes on the seventeenth, when forty-one names were said in a yard a stranger can walk into, and said one sentence: that is not a list, and he has been doing this four years and he could not sell that in any of them.** He left a slate on the table and went out of that gate at about two in the afternoon on the nineteenth. **A stay counted without the day of arrival is five days, being the fourteenth to the eighteenth. HE IS NOT IN THIS DISTRICT AND NOTHING IN THIS BLOCK SAYS WHERE HE WENT.**
- **The man of about twenty-nine who drives a cart.** Hauled for the man with a case from the thirteenth without being asked, and sent nine sacks down to the scale on the nineteenth and did not take any money for them. **He is not the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room with nothing over the door and not the man of about nineteen who counts, and a clerk entered that the three of them are three people in three trades inside two miles of each other and that the trade is the identifier and never carries the identification alone.**
- **The man of about twenty-three who sells nothing and copies for nothing.** **Said yes to eleven coppers a week in about one second on the thirteenth of the sixth month, having been refused it eight times in five months, and gave the reason: a man with a case has not spent five months making him a refusal first, and being a refusal has never cost him anything.** He wrote a list in about an hour and his hand cramped. He said in a yard on the sixteenth that he had been in the room where the books are on the afternoon of the seventh and that the second book was open on the shelf and that he did not read it and has not stopped seeing it. **He said forty-one names out loud in the yard on the seventeenth. He could not hold a pen on the eighteenth. The arrangement was finished on the eighteenth in about four seconds. He said the whole of it out loud in a yard on the twentieth and then sat down. HE IS NOT PAID AND HIS HAND DOES NOT CLOSE.**
- **A man of about nineteen who counts.** Counted the people in a yard at ten on the sixteenth and got six and said a smaller number in a yard is not a smaller yard. Counted nine mats and stopped. **Counted the forty-one names on the seventeenth, and a clerk entered that he counted them and that nobody asked him to, and that a man who counts a thing when there is a number in front of him is not a counter of people and is nobody's clerk.**
- **The woman of fifty-eight.** **She has carried water to eleven houses for nineteen years and nobody in this district has ever asked her anything, and her buckets are about eleven pounds full and nobody counted them on any of the ten days of this block either.** She was asked the question about the ninth house on the fourth of this month and did not answer, and on the twelfth, and on the sixteenth, and on the eighteenth. **On the nineteenth she put a bucket down and sat on the step of two stones outside the second house up that lane, which nobody in this district had ever seen her do, and said thirteen words: I SAID IT BECAUSE YOU ASKED ME FIVE TIMES AND I AM FIFTY-EIGHT. And then she said the rest: the woman in the ninth of the eleven houses is not one of the four hundred and eleven, and the woman asked for a second bucket in the second week of last month, and a person who asks for a second bucket is a person who has answered a question, and it is not a lie and it is not a mistake.**
- **The man of about thirty at a counter.** The sixteenth time down the eleven mile road and the next is the seventeenth, and no chapter in this block gives him a day in this district.
- **The man of about forty-one who keeps a road house at the top of the road that climbs.** He has no day in this district, this district has never once given him one, and this block gave him none.
- **The man with the portfolio.** Still not in this district, off the page, and nothing in this block says where he is.

## The counts, and the seven that were defended

**Things this district does not have five, the fifth being a way to pay a person who is not in a household, and it was offered by a man with a case in a yard on the thirteenth of the sixth month and refused, and it is still not paid and not priced. THE STANDING FIGURE IS EIGHT REFUSALS OF ELEVEN COPPERS A WEEK AND IT DID NOT MOVE, and a man of about twenty-three saying yes on the thirteenth and being finished on the eighteenth is not a refusal this district made. Instruments built and not named six, and a seventh WAS proposed in this block and refused on the page, six candidates in all. Documents nobody owns three, and four documents arrived or were made in this block and none became a fourth. Protected things five and no sixth, and a building with two doors and a building with a hole in the end wall of it are both used as things that are not a sixth. Conditions with no end on it four. Refusals to join two things thirteen and the registrar's own count twelve, and neither moved, and no join was proposed in this block. Refusals to read nine, the departure unspent, no date in the ledger, the fourth line of the offer not read out, and the rival record is not the fourth line of the offer. Boards five and lines full six, a seventh ruled and empty, and nobody put a line on a board on any of the eighty-one days to the twentieth of this month, counting the first of the fourth month in. Columns of not-askings four, and a fifth was proposed on a printed sheet and refused on the page. A rate turning a year into coppers: none, and there never has been one, and eleven coppers was counted out onto a trestle table in a yard and a clerk entered that a cut is not a rate. Readings of the rival record seven, and the eighth was refused out loud in Chapter 251 of Batch 0001 and was not read out again. A chalk figure on a board, a set of doors counted in chalk, a subtraction of a printed number and two open pages, a list of people who are not in a household, a slate on a trestle table, a column for the person who keeps a yard, a pail carried off a man, about nine mats and a candle stub, and a bench with eleven notches offered to a man with a case were all refused on the page as instruments, columns or figures.**

**THE POOL OF REFUSALS WITH NO REASON A CLERK OF A HOUSE HAS GIVEN IS EIGHT AND IT STOOD WHERE IT WAS ON EVERY ONE OF THE TEN DAYS AGAINST SEVEN SEPARATE ATTEMPTS.** Seven people refused something with a reason and not one of them is a clerk of a house: **the woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale (261, 262 and 263), the man of about twenty-three who copies for nothing (261, 263 and 268), the woman of about thirty-four who keeps a goat (264), the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room with nothing over the door (264), the woman of about thirty-one who keeps a route (264 and 267), the man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally (265) and the man of about thirty-four who keeps a road (265).** A clerk entered at the point of each refusal with a reason that a refusal with a reason is not one of the eight and that a man or a woman who keeps a house is not a clerk of a house, and the eight did not move seventeen times across the ten chapters. The refusals about the ninth holding are seven and did not move and the two are not the same figure.

NO SENTENCE WAS SHORTENED TO MAKE A FIGURE TRUE.** The block total is 24,117 words by `wc -w` and 24,009 on the Batch 0005 denominator, and all ten chapters sit inside the 2,200 to 3,200 band.

## The world in this block

**A yard a stranger can walk into with a sheet about four hundred and eleven and a second sheet about one copy of anything on its gatepost, a trestle table with a slate on the end of it and nothing written on the slate, a chair nobody has sat in for fifty-eight days, and a gate that takes three goes. A ditch about two feet deep in the sixth month. A room about nine feet by fourteen with nothing over the door and about eleven notches on the underside of a bench and about nine people in it most nights, and a woman at a house on that lane who told a stranger where it was. A room about nine feet by eleven with one table, one shelf, one door, a fanlight over the door that lights nothing and has not in four months, and now two books. An oak frame four miles out past the loams with a cracked bell and nine inches of rope. A rise a mile out past the loams and a building eleven miles off, not mentioned in this block. A kitchen doorway four hundred yards down a lane with no chalk on the step and bread on a board. Four miles of road with nine houses on it, one of which went and found the man with the case. A gate on a rise a mile out past the loams and seven miles of road past it that nobody has walked in six years. A slate with twenty-four words on it.**

# Volume 06 Batch 0003 — Continuity, Chapters 271 to 280

**Additive to every section above. Volume 06 is open. The figures below are the state at Chapter 280 and Batch 0004 inherits them.**

## The instruments, and the four this block added or refused

**THE THREE THINGS A BELL HAS TO HAVE BEFORE THIS DISTRICT WILL CALL A SOUNDING SAFE, ENTERED IN ONE HAND AND NOT AS A PARAGRAPH IN CHAPTER 273, AND ALL THREE OF THEM ABSENT OR UNMET IN THIS DISTRICT AT THE END OF THE BLOCK.** A bell that is rung records everybody who hears it for one night. A bell rung when there is nothing to answer it cannot be rung again for a month. A community has to provide a shelter and a way to take the record off. **THE BLOCK PROVIDES A SHELTER AND A WAY TO TAKE A PIECE OF PAPER OFF A TABLE, AND WHAT CAME OFF WAS A RECORD OF A ROOM AND NOT THE RECORD THE BELL MADE, AND THIS DISTRICT CANNOT SAY WHETHER THE NIGHT OF THE TWENTY-FOURTH WAS A SOUNDING OR A BELL RUNG WITH NOTHING TO ANSWER IT, AND THE ONLY REASON IT CANNOT SAY IS THE FIFTH LINE OF THE CHARTER.**

- **A public entrance on a building with two doors.** 272. A curtain off the front door, back on the nail in the end of the end wall, in about four minutes. **A clerk entered at the point it was proposed that it is not a seventh instrument of the six and that the six did not move on the twenty-second of the sixth month, and that a thing that is there on one night and not on the next is not an instrument this district has built and named.**
- **A table with four legs against the outside wall of that building, a piece of chalk, and a piece of paper folded in four.** 274. **A man of about thirty-four who digs loam said out loud in a yard on the twenty-eighth that this and a man in the doorway taking a piece of paper off it is a counter, and that he has not got an argument against that and has not got an argument for it either. A table with four legs is not a counter and is not a market and is not a page and is not one of the three documents this district does not own.**
- **The lot about the room.** 278. Four lines, three in the hand of the man of about twenty-three and the fourth in the hand of the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing because he could not hold a pen. Read out in a yard and read back. Reproduced in full in `outline/batches/volume-06-batch-0003.md` section 3.1.
- **The charter for the building with two doors.** 279. Five lines, in the registrar's own hand with one word in another man's hand because hers stopped. Read out in a room with a shut door and read back by a woman of fifty-eight. Reproduced in full in the canon card at section 3.2. **IT IS WRONG ON ITS FACE.**
- **Refused on the page in this block, and the six did not move:** a board with four lines on it and a space at the bottom of it about the width of a finger for a name, refused at 272; a stool outside that door, refused at 272; a hand that stopped in the middle of a word, entered at 279. **Six candidates and none became a seventh.**

## The world in this block

**A yard a stranger can walk into with a sheet about four hundred and eleven and a second sheet about one copy of anything on its gatepost, a trestle table, a chair nobody has sat in for sixty-eight days, a gate that takes two goes, and a slate on the end of the table with nothing on it. A lane behind the bank about two hundred yards long with a wire on the left not tight since before anybody in this district was born, a step of two stones outside the second house, a third house up from the scale where a woman keeps a goat, a lean-to with a scale under it, and at the near end a building with two doors: a front door with a handle, about nine feet of brick with no window in it, a plank door in the end wall with a nail in a hasp, a curtain on a nail, and a table with four legs against the outside wall with a piece of chalk on it. A ditch about two feet deep in the sixth month. A room about two miles the other way with nothing over the door, about nine people most nights, a bench about nine feet long with about eleven notches cut in the underside of the seat at intervals that are not even, and a child of about eight asleep on it. A room about nine feet by eleven with one table, one shelf, two books, a door, and a fanlight that lights nothing. An oak frame four miles out past the loams about eleven feet high with a cracked bell, a headstock of iron, a newer frame about nine feet back holding nothing, and about three feet of rope on the bell now, which is the nine inches that were inside the beam tied to about two feet of new rope, where there were about nine inches inside the beam and nothing anybody standing on the ground could reach. A rise a mile out past the loams and a building eleven miles off. A kitchen doorway four hundred yards down a lane. Four miles of road with nine houses on it. A gate on a rise a mile out past the loams. A step of two stones outside the second house up the lane.**

## The people, and what changed in the block

**THE CHILD OF ABOUT EIGHT IS NEW TO THE PROSE IN TWO HUNDRED AND SEVENTY CHAPTERS.** He sleeps in the room with nothing over the door. He was awake at about half past three in the morning on the night of the twenty-fourth and did not go back to sleep. He heard the bell. He is not in the piece of paper, he is not in the lot, he is not in the charter, and he is not in any book, and **he has never been asked anything by anybody in this district in his life and a clerk entered that twice on purpose and said that the two sentences are the same sentence.** He is about eight years old and he is described and never named, and no chapter in this block asks him anything. **HE IS NOT THE BOY OF NINE WHOSE NAME IS IN A COLUMN IN A BUILDING TWO DAYS OF ROAD AWAY, AND THE TWO ARE NOT THE SAME PERSON AND ARE NOT ADDED TOGETHER.**

**A man of about thirty-four who digs loam said no in about four seconds on the twenty-first and was asked again in the same words and said yes, and named a price, and held a doorway from about half past one in the morning on the night of the twenty-fourth and took a piece of paper off a table in four goes with a hand that has not closed since the ditch behind that building on the eleventh, and the second go put it on the ground, and he said so out loud in the lane himself and nobody asked him about it.** A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing went up a frame about eleven feet high on the twenty-third with his right hand, took the skin off the inside of his left forearm from the wrist to the elbow on a headstock of iron, said so out loud in a yard eleven minutes later, pulled the bell at about two in the morning on the twenty-fourth, wrote the fourth line of the lot, read both documents out, and said in a lane on the thirtieth that a wrong sheet is a worse thing than no sheet. A man of fifty-six walked eight miles in the dark on the night of the twenty-fourth and got to the frame about two minutes after the bell stopped. A man of about twenty-three who copies for nothing lost his voice at about the sixth of nine names in a yard on the twenty-fifth and could not speak above a whisper for two days. A man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally held a page open for about an hour and could not afterwards see a figure at the length of his own arm. A woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room with nothing over the door said out loud in a yard that there is a child in that room and he heard the bell and he did not come. A woman of fifty-eight read a five-line charter back in a room and stopped at the fifth of them. **A man of about twenty-nine who drives a cart read two documents nobody asked him to read and said in a yard that the second of them is not true, and ended by saying that he is a man who drives a cart and that he is not one of the things he is talking about.** The woman of about thirty-one who keeps a route is in this block in one clerk's entry only, in the sentence that says she was not in that yard and does not come to that frame.

## The counts, and the eight that were defended

**Things this district does not have five, the fifth being a way to pay a person who is not in a household, and it was NOT PROPOSED IN THIS BLOCK AND IS NOT PAID AND NOT PRICED, and the reason it is not paid is on the page: the man whose offer fitted that hole exactly went out of that gate on the nineteenth of the sixth month with a list, and a man of about twenty-nine who drives a cart hauled nine sacks for him for nothing. Instruments built and not named six, and a public entrance was proposed twice and entered as not a seventh and two other candidates were refused on the page, five candidates in all, two refused and three entered as not a seventh. Documents nobody owns three, and four documents were made or arrived in this block and none became a fourth, because a document a person in this district wrote in their own hand in a yard in front of about nineteen people is a document this district owns, and a wall is not a page and a reprint of a wall is not a document. Protected things five and no sixth, and a bell is not a sixth and a person standing in a doorway is not a sixth. Conditions with no end on it four. Refusals to join two things thirteen and the registrar's own count twelve, and neither moved, and no join was proposed in this block. Refusals to read nine, the departure unspent, no date in the ledger, the fourth line of the offer not read out, and the rival record is not the fourth line of the offer and its eighth reading is not a ninth. Boards five and lines full six, a seventh ruled and empty at the ninth place, and nobody put a line on a board on any of the ninety-one days to the thirtieth of this month, counting the first of the fourth month in. Columns of not-askings four, and a fifth was proposed on a board on the twenty-second of this month and refused on the page. A rate turning a year into coppers: none, and there never has been one. Readings of the rival record seven, and the eighth reading was refused out loud on the twenty-fourth of this month with a reason and a refused reading is not a reading. A chalk figure on a board, a set of doors counted in chalk, a subtraction of a printed number and two open pages, a list of people who are not in a household, a slate on a trestle table, a table with four legs, a stool, a board with a finger's width at the bottom of it, and a hand that stopped in the middle of a word: NONE of them became a seventh instrument and NONE of them became a fourth of the three documents nobody owns.**

**THE POOL OF REFUSALS WITH NO REASON A CLERK OF A HOUSE HAS GIVEN IS EIGHT AND IT STOOD WHERE IT WAS ON EVERY ONE OF THE TEN DAYS AGAINST EIGHT SEPARATE ATTEMPTS.** Eight people refused something with a reason and not one of them is a clerk of a house: **the man of about thirty-four who digs loam (271, 277, 278 and 279), the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing (272, 276 and 277), the woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale (272 and 276), the man of about thirty-four who keeps a road (273 and 276), the woman of about thirty-four who keeps a goat (273), the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room with nothing over the door (275 and 277), the man of about twenty-three who copies for nothing (275), and the woman of fifty-eight (278).** A clerk entered at the point of each refusal with a reason that a refusal with a reason is not one of the eight and that a man or a woman who keeps a house is not a clerk of a house, and the eight did not move, and that entry was made once in each of ten days and not as a standing rule. The refusals about the ninth holding are seven and did not move and the two are not the same figure and are not added together.

## The calendar of this block, and the four relative months that were wrong in the first draft

**Chapters 271 to 280 are the twenty-first to the THIRTIETH OF THE SIXTH MONTH, ten days for ten chapters, one chapter a day, and no chapter in the block carries two dates. THE BLOCK DOES NOT CROSS INTO THE SEVENTH MONTH, AND CHAPTERS 281 TO 290 ARE THEREFORE THE FIRST TO THE ELEVENTH OF THE SEVENTH.** The sixth month has thirty days, the fifth thirty-one, the fourth thirty, the third thirty-one, the first thirty-one, the seventh thirty-one, and the second had twenty-eight, and **the length of the eighth month is not stated in this canon and was not used, and Chapters 271 and 280 both say so on the page.** Every interval was walked out of those seven month lengths in a scratch calculation before it was written down.

**Board 179 to 188, and the three derivations agree on every day. Train 495 to 504. Unentered days 209 to 218. The second of January 170 to 179. The first day of the month after next, which is the first of the eighth month, 41 to 32, counted as the days to the end of the sixth month plus the thirty-one of the seventh plus the first of the eighth, and the first term is nil on the thirtieth because the thirtieth of the sixth month is its last day. The ninth of the nine printed nights 62 to 71 days back. The chair 59 to 68 days. The six households 61 to 70 days. The pool of refusals with no reason is two months back and 89 days on the twenty-first. The days since anybody wrote on a board 82 to 91, counting the first of the fourth month in. The rival record in force 20 to 29 days.**

**FOUR RELATIVE-MONTH PHRASES WERE WRONG IN THIS BLOCK'S FIRST DRAFT AND ALL FOUR ARE CORRECTED, AND THE CHECK THAT CAUGHT ALL FOUR IS A SEPARATE READ OF EVERY PHRASE AGAINST THE MONTH IT NAMES AND NOT A CHECK OF ANY LADDER.** Chapter 271 counted the days since anybody wrote on a board as running *since the first of the month before that*, which read as it stands is the fifth month and gives fifty-two days and not the eighty-two on the page; corrected to *the first of the fourth month*, **and that one is the worst of the four because the figure it belongs to was internally consistent in the other nine chapters.** Chapter 272's registrar said she had said on the twentieth of *last month* that she wanted to have had nothing to do with what he was going to do, and she said it on the twentieth of the sixth month, which from the first of the sixth month is two days back and not a month; corrected. Chapter 276's woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale said she had refused her four empty houses *twice this month*, and the page has one refusal, on the first, and her own statement on the twentieth that they are not going to be offered again; corrected. Chapter 279's woman of fifty-eight said the second week of the fifth month was *the month before last*, and from the twenty-ninth of the sixth month the fifth month is *last month*; corrected. **AND THE LESSON IS THE ONE THE VOLUME HAS BEEN WRITING SINCE BATCH 0001: A LADDER CHECK CANNOT SEE A PHRASE, AND A COUNT OF DAYS THAT NAMES ITS OWN START IN A RELATIVE PHRASE IS A COUNT OF DAYS THAT CAN BE TWO FIGURES.**

## The counting motif, thirty-two class-one claims and five class-two claims

**Thirty-two class-one claims in the class counts 4, 4, 2, 3, 3, 4, 4, 2, 3, 3, and thirty-two reproduce, with a mismatch count of zero. Five class-two claims, ten, fifteen, sixty-one, sixteen and seventy-four, each against a printed span of the same length, and all five reproduce.** The detector was calibrated on Chapters 241 to 250 first and returns 53 class-one claims, 0 mismatches, a denominator of 25,569, a `wc -w` total of 25,689 and 675 shared twelve-word runs, **all five of which are the figures the Volume 05 records print and all five of which reproduce.** **Every one of the thirty-two moved at least once while the block was being written, five moved twice, a review pass moved four more, and every one was re-measured on the printed sentence rather than re-aimed. THE FOUR THAT MOVED IN REVIEW WERE 262 TO 263, 172 TO 169, 176 TO 193 AND 175 TO 185, AND ALL FOUR MOVED BECAUSE A MISATTRIBUTED QUOTATION, A ROPE MEASURED AT A THIRD OF ITS LENGTH, A MAN TAKING CREDIT FOR ANOTHER MAN'S ARGUMENT AND AN INVENTED SCENE WERE CUT OUT OF THE SENTENCES THEY SIT ON. NO SENTENCE IN CHAPTERS 271 TO 280 WAS SHORTENED TO MAKE A FIGURE TRUE AND NO FIGURE WAS CHANGED TO FIT A SENTENCE.** The block total is 26,854 words by `wc -w` and 26,733 on the Batch 0005 denominator, and all ten chapters sit inside the 2,200 to 3,200 band. **ZERO SYSTEM PANELS IN THIS BLOCK AGAINST A CAP OF TWO.**

## Untouched in this block, to be left untouched

**The blank lot, Iven, the ninth design signature, the origin of the Common Measure, the First House, the removed signature, the three unexplained things of Volume 01, the office, the resolution of the romance, the name of the city on the agent's ledger, the name of the agent in any register in the west bank, the fourth line of the offer, the departure, the boy's name in a column in a building two days of road away, the two figures of five years and nine years, the iron, the echo of an origin toll, the Last Fare, the market stalls on the lower terrace, the first channel's failure and its sills, the four gaps, the two impressions, the two lines holding the office's name, the four lines of the broken circle, the signal's memory pattern, the pan that cannot report itself, the place that cannot report itself, and the mark on the iron. No place is named on any document in this district and none is named in narration, the country above the water is not named, and the Hall is named once in narration as a building. Cael Orin's name was said once in a yard in Chapter 203 and was not said a second time. Selik Marne is a wary ally and not a friend and is not in this block. THE LOCK COUNCIL AND THE QUIET REGISTRY ARE BODIES THAT PRINT AND DO NOT ARRIVE, AND NEITHER ACQUIRED A FACE. A KEEPER HAS NEVER BEEN ON A PAGE AND THERE NEVER WAS ONE AND NO KEEPER IS NAMED, AND THE BARE WORD OCCURS THIRTY-ONE TIMES AND EVERY OCCURRENCE IS A DENIAL, A PRINTED RULE ABOUT A JOB, A COLUMN THAT IS EMPTY, OR A NOUN SOMEBODY HAS BEEN TOLD THEY ARE NOT. THE NINTH PLACE ON THE PAGE OUT OF THE CAVE IS RULED AND EMPTY AND WAS NOT FILLED. THE NINTH CHARTER'S LINE IS EMPTY. THE THIRD COLUMN OF THE COVENANT IS EMPTY AND WAS NOT FILLED AND A MAN NAMED ITS SHAPE OUT LOUD IN A YARD ON THE TWENTY-SIXTH AND IT WAS NOT JOINED TO ANYTHING. THE NINTH OF THE NINE NIGHTS WAS NAMED IN FIVE CHAPTERS AND CLOSED IN NONE. THE RIGHT NOT TO BE LISTED IS NOT A TERM. NO WRONG COUNT WAS STRUCK, AND THE RULE THAT A WRONG COUNT AND A RIGHT COUNT GO IN THE SAME ENTRY WITH THE REASON UNDERNEATH IS THE CLIMAX'S RULE AND IT IS CHAPTERS 296 TO 299. THE WOMAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-ONE WHO KEEPS A ROUTE IS NOT IN THIS DISTRICT ON ANY OF THE TEN DAYS AND HER EXIT CLAUSE IS NOT SPENT. THE RECORD THAT THE BELL MADE IS NOT IN THIS BLOCK AND THE NEXT VOLUME'S QUESTION IS NOT FORESHADOWED BEYOND ONE SENTENCE IN ONE DOCUMENT. NO NEW FINAL ENEMY.**

# Volume 06 Batch 0004 — Continuity, Chapters 281 to 290

**Additive to every section above. Volume 06 is open. The figures below are the state at Chapter 290 and Batch 0005 inherits them.**

## The instruments, and the one that was refused on the page and the six that were entered

**THE OFFER, FIVE LINES, IN A HAND-CART, READ OUT AND READ BACK IN THE YARD OF LOT SEVENTEEN ON THE SECOND OF THE SEVENTH MONTH.** Reproduced in full in `outline/batches/volume-06-batch-0004.md` section 3.1. **THE FIFTH OF ITS FIVE LINES IS WRONG AND THE WRITER OF IT SAID OUT LOUD IN THE SAME YARD THAT HE WROTE IT ON PURPOSE: A PERSON WHO HAS STOOD AT THAT TABLE ONCE CANNOT BE UNCOUNTED AGAIN. IT IS THE SAME SHAPE OF WRONGNESS AS THE FIFTH LINE OF THE SHELTER'S CHARTER, AND THE TWO ARE NOT JOINED AND WERE NOT JOINED IN THAT YARD, AND A CLERK ENTERED THAT.**

- **THE LINE OF PRINT, FOUR LINES, NO NAME AND NO SEAL, BROUGHT IN ON THE SAME HAND-CART ON THE FOURTH.** Reproduced in full in the canon card at 3.2. **It was asked for and not read out in that yard, and the second of its four lines is a rule about an address.** It names no body and found nobody and creates no consent and makes no person a bearer.
- **A TABLE UNDER A LEAN-TO ON THE FAR SIDE OF THE ELEVEN MILE ROAD.** 282, 283, 287, 290. **There is no lean-to there. There is a wall about four feet high with a gate in it that is not on a hinge, and a field about two hundred yards across, and a ditch about three feet deep in the seventh month where it was about two feet deep in the sixth.** A man of about thirty-seven set a table against that wall on the seventh of the seventh month at about eight in the morning and there was one person standing at it and he did not describe that person to this district.
- **THE ONE THING REFUSED ON THE PAGE IN THIS BLOCK, ON THE FIFTH OF THE SEVENTH MONTH.** A join: the offer's five lines and the four lines of print put on one page and read out together, proposed by the man of about thirty-seven who puts tables up for anybody who will use them and refused in about four seconds with a reason by the man of about thirty-four who keeps a road, and a clerk entered that a join is a join and is not a seventh of the instruments. **Six further things entered as not a seventh, as the ledger at 290 names them: a table on a cart; a copy in the hand of a man whose hand has not closed since the thirteenth of last month; a panel, 286; a sheet of four lines of print, 284; a wall about four feet high; and a man with a cart. SEVEN THINGS NAMED AT 290, ONE REFUSED ON THE PAGE AND SIX ENTERED, AND NONE BECAME A SEVENTH. THERE IS NO SUCH THING AS A SECOND HAND-CART IN THE PROSE; THE PROPOSAL THAT WAS REFUSED IS A JOIN, AND A HAND-CART IS THE THING THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-SEVEN ALREADY BROUGHT DOWN THE ELEVEN MILE ROAD WITH.**

## The world in this block

**A yard a stranger can walk into with two sheets on its gatepost, a trestle table, a chair nobody has sat in for seventy-eight days, a gate that takes two goes, and a hand-cart and a tent against the wall inside it. A lane behind a bank about two hundred yards long with a wire on the left not tight since before anybody in this district was born, a step of two stones outside the second house, a third house up from the scale where a woman keeps a goat, a lean-to with a scale under it, and at the near end a building with two doors: a front door with a handle, a plank door in the end wall with a nail in a hasp, a curtain on a nail, a table with four legs against the outside wall, and a ditch behind it that was about two feet deep in the sixth month and was about three feet deep on the fourth of the seventh with about a foot of standing water in the bottom of it. Eleven miles of road to a wall about four feet high with a gate in it that is not on a hinge, about a mile short of it a turning that is not on any sheet, and a field about two hundred yards across with a ditch at the far end of it. A room about nine feet by eleven with two books on a shelf, one of which has been open since the sixth of last month and was not read in this block. A room about two miles the other way with nothing over the door, which was not entered in this block. Four miles of road with nine houses on it. The top of a bank where a man of about thirty-seven and a man of fifty-six stood at half past four on the sixth of this month.**

## The people, and what changed in the block

**A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-SEVEN WHO PUTS TABLES UP FOR ANYBODY WHO WILL USE THEM IS NEW TO THE PROSE IN THREE HUNDRED CHAPTERS.** He came down the eleven mile road on the thirtieth of the sixth month with a hand-cart and a tent rolled on it, gave no name to anybody, was not asked for one, asked the yard of Lot Seventeen for its address, put a five-line offer on the trestle table, had it read out and read back, agreed out loud with a man about the wrongness of its own fifth line, dug a copy's worth of a page he could not finish, named eleven coppers a week in a yard, refused a figure of two mornings, carried two trestle boards up a bank on the first of this month on a shoulder, and **on the tenth of this month could not lift a board off his own cart with either hand and said so out loud in about nine seconds of standing there before anybody worked it out of him.** He is not a keeper of anything and has never been asked to be one.

**A woman of about thirty-one who keeps a route for people who are not in any book came down four miles on the ninth of the seventh month and went back up them an hour later, and was asked one thing in a lane, and refused it with a reason she has never given anybody — the eleven years are how long she has walked her lines, and they are not how old the reason is, and an earlier draft of this line made the reason eleven years old, which the prose does not say — and told nobody anything else.** The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room with nothing over the door is not in this block except as the room a woman of about thirty-four carried a copy up two miles to read in. The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale said the word rate out loud in a yard before anybody asked her for it. The woman of fifty-eight was looked at by four people in a yard and answered a question about a house in the ground herself. The man of about twenty-nine who drives a cart read the offer back in a yard and hauled a table eleven miles twice for nothing. The man of about twenty-three who sells nothing and copies for nothing got through two lines of a copy, could not do the third, and read four lines of print out loud in a lane because a thing that is only read by one man in a yard stops when that man is ill. The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally walked down the lane with his book and said nothing but good morning. The man of about thirty-four who digs loam walked eleven miles out and back in about eleven hours, put his hand in a ditch for an hour, spent an afternoon at the bottom of a three foot ditch and had to be helped out of it, and lifted a trestle board off a hand-cart at the end of the block.

## The counts, and the one that moved and why

**Things this district does not have five, the fifth being a way to pay a person who is not in a household, and the NAME OF THE FIFTH THING WAS SAID OUT LOUD IN THE YARD OF LOT SEVENTEEN ON THE THIRD OF THE SEVENTH MONTH BY A MAN WHO HAS IT TO SELL, AND SAYING IT DID NOT MAKE IT. Instruments built and not named six, and **NOTHING IN THIS BLOCK BECAME A SEVENTH. THE ONE THING REFUSED ON THE PAGE WAS A JOIN, THE OFFER'S FIVE LINES AND THE FOUR LINES OF PRINT ON ONE PAGE, ON THE FIFTH, AND A CLERK ENTERED THAT A JOIN IS A JOIN AND IS NOT A SEVENTH OF THE INSTRUMENTS, AND SIX FURTHER THINGS WERE ENTERED AS NOT A SEVENTH. A SECOND HAND-CART WAS NAMED HERE AND IN THE CARD AND IS IN NO CHAPTER, AND IT IS WITHDRAWN.** Documents nobody owns three, and two documents arrived in this block and neither became a fourth of them. Protected things five and no sixth, and an address is not a sixth and a lean-to is not a sixth and a table is not a sixth and a panel is not a sixth. Conditions with no end on it four. Refusals to join two things thirteen and the registrar's own count twelve, and neither moved, and ONE JOIN WAS PROPOSED IN THIS BLOCK AND REFUSED ON THE PAGE. Refusals to read nine, the departure unspent, no date in the ledger, the fourth line of the offer not read out. Boards five and lines full six, a seventh ruled and empty at the ninth place, and nobody put a line on a board on any of the hundred and one days to the tenth of this month, counting the first of the fourth month in. Columns of not-askings four and a clerk entered on four separate days in this block that she did not rule one. A rate turning a year into coppers: none, and there never has been one, AND ELEVEN COPPERS A WEEK IS A PRICE A MAN NAMED OUT LOUD AND IS NOT A RATE. Readings of the rival record seven, and the eighth reading was refused out loud on the twenty-fourth of last month with a reason and it is unanswered.**

**THE FIGURE THAT MOVED IN THIS BLOCK IS THE REFUSALS OF ELEVEN COPPERS A WEEK, WHICH ARE NINE AND WERE EIGHT.** It moved on the third of the seventh month, in a yard, in front of about nineteen people, by a refusal with a reason, from a man who is not a clerk of a house. **THE EIGHT REFUSALS WITH NO REASON A CLERK OF A HOUSE HAS GIVEN ARE EIGHT AND DID NOT MOVE ON ANY OF THE TEN DAYS, AND A CLERK ENTERED THE DIFFERENCE AT THE POINT IT MOVED, ON THE PAGE, IN ONE HAND, WITH BOTH FIGURES IN THE SAME SENTENCE, AND SAID THAT NEITHER FIGURE IS A NINTH OR A TENTH OF THE OTHER.** **EIGHT PEOPLE SAID NO TO SOMETHING IN THIS BLOCK AND NOT ONE OF THEM IS A CLERK OF A HOUSE: the man of about thirty-four who digs loam (283), the man of about thirty-four who keeps a road (285), the man of about thirty-seven who puts tables up (283, 289), the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing (288), the woman of about thirty-four who keeps a goat (287), the woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale (283), the woman of about fifty-eight (286), and the woman of about thirty-one who keeps a route (289). EIGHT PEOPLE IS A LIST OF NAMES AND NOT A FIGURE, AND IT IS NOT THE FIGURE THE EIGHT IS, AND THE TWO ARE NOT ADDED TOGETHER. AN EARLIER DRAFT OF THIS SECTION READ *AGAINST EIGHT SEPARATE ATTEMPTS*, WHICH PUTS THE STANDING FIGURE INTO THE PLACE OF A COUNT OF WHAT HAPPENED; THE BLOCK'S OWN GUARDRAIL SAYS A PERSON WHO HAS NOT BEEN ASKED HAS NOT REFUSED AND CANNOT BE COUNTED EITHER WAY, AND SEVERAL OF THE EIGHT ABOVE WERE NEVER ASKED AND SAID IT FIRST, SO THE COUNT OF THINGS THAT CAN BE COUNTED AS REFUSALS IN THIS BLOCK IS FEWER THAN EIGHT, AND THE ENTRY THAT SAYS A REFUSAL WITH A REASON IS NOT ONE OF THE EIGHT IS ON THE PAGE IN SIX OF THE TEN DAYS RATHER THAN IN TEN.** The refusals about the ninth holding are seven and did not move and **the seven, the eight and the nine are three different figures and are not added together.**

## The calendar of this block

**Chapters 281 to 290 are the first to the TENTH OF THE SEVENTH MONTH, ten days for ten chapters, one chapter a day, and no chapter in the block carries two dates.** The sixth month has thirty days, the fifth thirty-one, the fourth thirty, the third thirty-one, the first thirty-one, the seventh thirty-one, and the second had twenty-eight, **and the length of the eighth month is not stated in this canon and was not used, and Chapters 281 and 290 both say so on the page. Every interval was walked out of those seven month lengths in a scratch calculation before it was written down, AND THE SCRATCH CALCULATION CAUGHT TWO FIGURES IN THE PHASE PROMPT ITSELF, A TRAIN LADDER ONE HIGH AND A SPAN ONE DAY LONG, AND NEITHER IS USED AND BOTH ARE NAMED IN THE BLOCK RECORD.**

**Board 189 to 198, and the three derivations agree on every one of the ten days. Train 505 to 514, and the prompt's 506 and 516 do not re-derive. Unentered days 219 to 228. The second of January 180 to 189. The first day of the eighth month 31 to 22, counted as the days to the end of the seventh month plus the first of the eighth, and the whole ten-term ladder is printed out loud at 290. The ninth of the nine printed nights 72 to 81 days back. The chair 69 to 78 days. The six households 71 to 80 days, entered out of a ladder and not asked for. The pool of refusals with no reason a clerk of a house has given is three months back and 99 to 108 days. The days since anybody wrote on a board 92 to 101, counting the first of the fourth month in. The rival record in force 30 to 39 days.**

**TWO RELATIVE-MONTH PHRASES WERE WRONG IN THIS BLOCK'S FIRST DRAFT AND BOTH ARE CORRECTED, AND THE CHECK THAT CAUGHT THEM IS A SEPARATE READ OF EVERY PHRASE AGAINST THE MONTH IT NAMES.** Chapters 282 and 283 both said the man of about forty-four with a case paid a man of about twenty-three *in the month before last*; from the second and the third of the seventh month the sixth month is *last month*, and the payment was on the thirteenth of the sixth. **THE PHRASE THIS DISTRICT HAD USED FOR THE REMOVAL DAY SINCE THE FIRST OF THE SIXTH MONTH, *THE FIRST DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER NEXT*, MEANS THE NINTH MONTH FROM THE SEVENTH AND THE PROSE RETIRED IT ON THE FIRST DAY OF THE BLOCK, IN A CLERK'S ENTRY, IN ONE SENTENCE, AND IT OCCURS ONCE IN TEN CHAPTERS AND THAT SENTENCE IS THE OCCURRENCE.**

## The counting motif, twenty-five class-one claims and no class-two claims

## The world in this block

**A yard a stranger can walk into with a trestle table, a chair nobody has sat in for eighty-eight days, a gate that takes two goes, a hand-cart and a tent rolled on it against the wall inside the gate, a piece of paper with two lines of print on it lying on the end of the trestle table since the fifth of this month, a sheet of five lines of print on the end of the same table from the twelfth, a blanket of scale under a lean-to at the bank, and two sheets on a gatepost. A lane behind a bank about two hundred yards long, a step of two stones outside the second house, a third house up from the scale where a woman keeps a goat, and at the near end a building with two doors, about nine feet of brick with no window in it, a plank door in the end wall with a nail in a hasp that has not moved since the eleventh of last month, a curtain on a nail, a table with four legs against the outside wall, and a ditch behind it about three feet deep with about a foot of standing water in it. A room about nine feet by eleven with two books on a shelf, a shut door, a handle, a step of two stones, and a fanlight over the door that lights nothing. A room about two miles the other way with nothing over the door, about nine people in it and a bench with eleven notches cut in the underside of the seat, WHICH WAS NOT ENTERED IN THIS BLOCK. Eleven miles of road to a wall about four feet high with a gate in it that is not on a hinge, about a mile short of it a turning that is not on any sheet, and a field about two hundred yards across with a ditch about three feet deep at the far end of it.**
